‘He Walked So I Could Run’: ‘Project Hail Mary’ Breakout James Ortiz on Andy Serkis, Becoming Rocky and the Art of Puppetry

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Awards Circuit Podcast: The roundtable discusses “The Further Mis-Adventures of Cliff Booth” and the casting of Robert Pattinson as James Bond.

The reason so many of us cried at “Project Hail Mary” has five legs, no face and a voice that belongs to James Ortiz. And he’s so damn effective.

In April, Variety exclusively reported that Amazon MGM would submit Ortiz for best supporting actor at the 99th Academy Awards for his performance as Rocky. We’ve written plenty of consideration stories. None has produced what that one did: TikToks, Instagram Reels and a corner of film Twitter demanding, with real passion, that someone “get this adorable rock creature nominated for an Oscar immediately,” as Mike Sawicki of the “Mike, Mike and Oscar” YouTube channel and podcast put it. Related Stories Priyanka Chopra Jonas, Russell Crowe Sci-Fi Thriller 'Bluefly' Adds 'Beast' Star Daniel MacPherson (EXCLUSIVE) 'Beast in Me' Season 2 Casts Riley Keough, Lorelei Olivia Mote, Aaron Paul, Taip Ceman, Matthew James Thomas

The groundswell even made it to NBC’s “Today,” where Carson Daly spotlighted it.

Months later, sitting in the Variety Awards Circuit Podcast studio, Ortiz still sounds like he’s processing all of it. “Did this actually happen?” he says with a wince. “It feels like it happened to somebody else two years ago.”

On this episode, Ortiz talks about bringing Rocky to life through his puppet artistry, from the eight weeks his team spent turning subtext into movement to the hours of reference footage that guided Framestore’s animators. He also lays out what he wants from Hollywood when it comes to recognizing and protecting puppetry work, why Phil Lord and Chris Miller’s set felt like “putting on a play in their parents’ garage” and the “Batman Forever” obsession that shaped him as a kid.

Lord and Miller’s adaptation of Andy Weir’s novel opened in March to $80.5 million domestically, a record for Amazon MGM and went on to gross more than $680 million worldwide, the non-franchise phenomenon the theatrical business had been praying for. At its center, opposite Ryan Gosling’s Ryland Grace, sits Rocky, the Eridian engineer built by Neal Scanlan’s creature shop and performed on set by Ortiz and a team of puppeteers he assembled. He was hired to operate the character. The voice was supposed to come later, from someone more famous. At the 11th hour, it was his.

Born in Albany and raised in Texas, the Puerto Rican and Italian artist trained classically at Purchase College and found puppetry when the acting jobs didn’t come. His first agent told him he wouldn’t work until he was 40. He turned 40 a couple of months before cameras rolled. Onstage, he won an Obie for “The Woodsman,” designed the puppets for Lincoln Center’s “The Skin of Our Teeth” and created Milky White, the scene-stealing cow of the 2022 Broadway revival of “Into the Woods.” “Project Hail Mary” is his first film.

What strikes me is how plainly he describes the work as acting. He breaks down a script the way he once broke down Prospero and Oberon. The only difference, he says, is that he funnels those choices “through an external shell.” That’s why the conversation around him matters.

We’ve had versions of this debate before, with Frank Oz’s Yoda, Andy Serkis’ Gollum and Zoe Saldaña in “Avatar.” Ortiz is eligible for both the Oscars and the Actor Awards, where SAG-AFTRA covers puppeteers. In theater, he points out, puppeteers are most often classified as crew.

He isn’t campaigning for a trophy so much as for recognition of a craft. “The goal is to create a character that you get lost in,” he says. “But I think there’s value in talking about the folks that made that.”

Excerpts from the episode, edited and condensed for clarity, are below.

The way we approached making that thing, even though it was my first film ever, felt, from what I understand, quite avant-garde. In a funny way, it felt like I was doing some downtown play every day. But I left every day going, “I don’t really know what we just made.” There was such an emphasis on crafting and improvising on the take that there were so many different versions of every single second of that film. So two years of waiting was pretty brutal.

I was so pleased and surprised when I got to see the final thing, especially when, at the 11th hour, I was told I was going to be the voice. And then the studio and the team said, “Why don’t we talk about this?” That’s a really unusual thing for suit performers and mocap and puppeteers.

My goal was always to be an actor. I went through this classical training program at Purchase, and it was all Shakespeare and Chekhov, all the things I’m still really passionate about. Then it’s that funny thing: What do you do with that information? How do you sell cereal? How do you do a bite and smile?

The first agent I signed with, in my early 20s, the ink was literally still wet on the contract, said, “You know, you’re not going to work until you’re 40.” And I turned 40 a couple of months before we started shooting “Hail Mary.” Isn’t that nuts? So maybe he was right. Who cares?

I was always drawn to painting and drawing and sculpting. My father was always engineering and tinkering and building things in the house. My mother is an academic, really literary and taught me about story structure. Long story short, all of those elements go directly into puppetry. I loved acting, performing, directing, engineering and fine art and it felt like I gamed the system by finding something where I didn’t have to pick one. When the acting gigs weren’t coming in, one thing accidentally led to another. I said, “Yeah, I can do that for this wacky downtown show for $200″ and it kick-started this thing.

It’s become a way for me to play characters I would never, ever be cast as. That’s the thing I found about puppetry: It ends up being the most freeing form of acting because I don’t have to start from how you perceive me.

On any other role I’ve played, sans puppet, I would have ripped the script apart trying to figure out who this soul is. How do they think? How do they love? What are they terrified of? Then the exercise becomes how you funnel that through your human shell, physically and emotionally. The only difference with puppetry is that I’m funneling it through an external shell. I’m doing all of the homework.

My approach, maybe because I started as an actor, whether it was creating Milky White on Broadway or “The Skin of Our Teeth” for Lincoln Center or the operas I’m doing, was always: What do we want the audience to feel? The precision will come later. This is a tool to play a character that should not be played by a person. So let’s take advantage of it. Let’s not let it be pedestrian.

I was originally going to play the puppet entirely by myself on set. Then we saw the scale of it and how many legs he had. We knew that from the book, but seeing the puppet, I raised my hand and said, “Guys, I do a lot of ensemble puppetry and ensemble direction in my theatrical career. I would love to bring in other local puppet artists and build a team.” Not people who are just there to hold a leg. I needed to get them inside my head so we could play this character together.

For about eight weeks before we shot, we focused on how to use movement to express subtext. This is a character coming from a pretty traumatic 80 years alone in space. This character has never been alone before in their life. They’re a eusocial species, so they truly don’t know what it is to be solo. How does that existential pain manifest physically? That’s the delicious stuff. And I’m making it sound like we did a Bergman film. There was a lot of humor and silliness, but all of that has to come from something that feels grounded.

This might sound a little crazy, but there is no such thing, especially on film, as an actor’s performance being entirely their own. There are costume people and other artists collaborating with them to create this character. The way the DP shoots you, the way the editor crafts it, that creates the final character.

After we wrapped, I got to see a cut and Phil and Chris asked, “Do you have any thoughts?” I said, “Well, I do. Are you sure?” I sent about seven or eight pages of notes about who Rocky is and how he moves, so the animators had more information and Framestore implemented that.

When you’re getting a puppet on camera, you’re focused on the frame. But if they need to go back later and add a CG counterpart, what are they basing it off of? So I gave them hours and hours of me and my team with a smaller puppet covered in dots, playing full scenes without any breaks. It was a chance to throw in every good idea or any bad idea I had left that we wouldn’t have been able to shoot on the day.

My team and I had worked out little bits of funny business. There’s a moment when Rocky has an epiphany and runs from one place to the other, and he falls ass over teakettle down this ramp. That was something we did on the reference table. When he sits on the beach at the end, his little legs wrap around him like a cinnamon bun. One of my puppeteers said, “It’s so cute. I’m desperate to find a way to put this in there.” I went, “Yes, this is it.” And they did it.

It’s one of the few unions ever that recognizes puppetry as performance. Theatrically, there’s actually no puppet representation, union-wise. Most of the time, puppeteers are considered crew and that means a variety of other things.

Generally, when there’s on-set puppetry in a Star Wars movie or some big-budget genre thing, it’s been storyboarded within an inch of its life. The puppeteer is brought in: Here’s page five of scene six, he takes three steps and he turns. OK, that’s lunch. It’s much more technical. Phil and Chris and Ryan and all the producers were never interested in that. Working with Phil and Chris was such a, I’m trying not to curse, a friggin’ gift. It felt like being in a writers’ room, a fluid pitch meeting.

I don’t know if credit is the point, but I do think acknowledging the effort, acknowledging that C-3PO was actually Anthony Daniels, there’s something to that. The goal of all of this is to create a character that you completely believe, so you don’t think about the other things. But from an industry standpoint, there’s going to be more genre storytelling, more characters we currently cannot imagine. How do we keep having these conversations? I have to keep giving flowers to Amazon MGM because they said this was such an unusual way of working, so why don’t we talk about it?

He was such a touchstone for how we approached Rocky. I was of the age where I ate up all those “Lord of the Rings” docs and special features. There’s probably a two-hour doc just about Gollum. I logged it away years ago. He walked so I could run. I am standing on the shoulders of many giants. I did not invent anything. But sometimes it’s a huge gift that someone else came before you, and you can point at something and go, “Like this.”

It’s overwhelming. I can’t process any of it. Who knows if any of this conversation leads to more? I don’t even want to say awareness. It’s not like a disease. Get tested for rocks. But this art form has always been there. It’s always going to be there. Obviously, the goal is to create a character that you get lost in, but I think there’s value in talking about the folks that made that.

It’s a collaborative art form. There’s no version of it that happens by one person. Many singular people are on the poster at the end of the day, but that’s why the credits are so long. Every single one of those people bled and sweat and cried and probably got a divorce while they were making this thing.

As a kid, I was such a fan of “Batman Forever.” I was so nuts about Jim Carrey in that movie. It’s a great film, but there’s also a longer conversation there about the queer identity that I discovered in myself from that film, which is not rapid fire.

Oh, my God. Easy. I always run toward “Un-Break My Heart” by Toni Braxton, and occasionally, if we’re feeling frisky, I might do “Orange Colored Sky” by Natalie Cole.

“This Rock and This Man: What an Adventure!” It’s wordy, but I think it gets us there.

I can clap with one hand. That’s going to sound disgusting, I know. It’s pretty terrifying.

Probably my Apple Pencil, because I’m drawing all the time.

God, am I only going to talk about Batman? “Batman: The Animated Series.” It’s a show I still go back to all the time.

Honestly, the only thing that came to mind is Rocky.

Vikings Receive Sudden Injury News Before 49ers Game

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Minnesota Vikings have been dealing with a whole lot of injury issues recently, and now, another problem has surfaced before their Week 11 matchup with the San Francisco 49ers.

Vikings cornerback Isaiah Rodgers, who has developed into a crucial part of the team's secondary, is now questionable to play this Sunday as a result of a calf injury that limited him in practice both Thursday and Friday.

The development was revealed by Minnesota head coach Kevin O'Connell while speaking to reporters on Friday.

O'Connell certainly didn't seem overly pessimistic, but Rodgers' condition is obviously something to monitor heading into the Vikings' showdown in Mexico City.

"He was limited today. It felt pretty good," O'Connell said. "We'll see how he feels post-practice treatment and all that."

Dec 25, 2025; Minneapolis, Minnesota, USA; Minnesota Vikings head coach Kevin O'Connell looks on in the second quarter against the Detroit Lions at U.S. Bank Stadium. Mandatory Credit: Matt Krohn-Imagn Images

The Vikings looked fantastic during Kirk Cousins' debut against the Green Bay Packers last Sunday, coming away with a 30-10 win at Lambeau Field and completing a regular-season sweep of their NFC North rivals.

Now, Minnesota will have a chance to send another strong message this weekend.

The 49ers are 8-1 and are obvious Super Bowl contenders, so this matchup could potentially represent an NFC Championship Game preview. If the Vikings are able to come away with another win, their title hopes will be taken that much more seriously.

Minnesota acquired Cousins before the NFL trade deadline, bringing him back for his second stint in the Twin Cities. He initially played for the Vikings between 2018 and 2023, but failed to lead the Vikings to a Super Bowl appearance.

That could certainly change this year, as Minnesota boasts a dominant defense and a plethora of weapons offensively.

A victory over San Francisco would go a long way in proving that the Vikings are, in fact, for real.

Why 30 Ideas for Fixing U.S. Schools Missed the Plot

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 20, 2026 | Reviewed by Tyler Woods

In September 2026, the New York Times published an article titled “30 Ideas for Fixing US Schools, Ranked by Experts” to address the problem of declining literacy and math rates across the United States. The experts identified solutions based on effectiveness and relative cost, highlighting the top five solutions as small reading groups, science of reading, universal pre-K, increased funding, and increased teacher pay for under-resourced schools and communities.

To be clear, these ideas are backed by science and arguably would be welcomed policy changes for supporting U.S. students.

But even these interventions on their own will continue to fail students if we don’t address the bigger underlying concern: Schools were never designed for children’s development.

The model of our public education system was informed by an 18th-century Prussian military regime with the intended goal of preparing students for work in factories. The United States, deep into the Industrial Revolution, needed workers who could follow instructions, keep a schedule, and perform quality work. U.S. public schools were a solution to overcrowded, one-room schoolhouses and the need to support a new workforce.

It’s no wonder then that reform efforts date back literally to the inception of public education itself. While lots of positive changes have come from those efforts, including accommodations for students with disabilities and scientific advancements in how to educate, the problem persists: Schools don’t align with child development.

The research is clear on what works for children, especially young learners from preschool through eight years of age. Children learn best:

The harsh reality is that when developmentally appropriate practice is stripped from education, then all the best curricula in the world will have minimal effects, at best. The list of 30 best ideas for fixing U.S. public schools? That is phase 2.

We must begin with the root cause of the challenges we are seeing. The kids are letting us know in no uncertain terms that they are done with the 18th-century structure that wasn’t even designed for them in the first place.

Put child development experts at the helm. Reimagine public education from the ground up for kids, not in spite. When we let them learn from a developmental perspective, then we leverage the innate gifts and talents our children already have. They want to learn. They have the capacity to think deeply and creatively. The problem is that we have removed every ounce of developmentally appropriate learning from the schools. And then we wonder why it isn’t working.

Transform the environment. Bring in child development research to guide the way. That is where we begin. That is where we will find the solutions.

Mashburn, A. J., Justice, L. M., Downer, J. T., & Pianta, R. C. (2009). Peer effects on children’s language achievement during pre-kindergarten. Child development, 80(3), 686-702.

Mexico’s President Spent 21 Hours In Coach Getting To China—And Sat In A Middle Seat

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Mexico’s President Claudia Sheinbaum spent about 21 hours getting to China in economy on China Southern Airlines. She reportedly sat in 33B. A middle seat, on a trip across the Pacific, to represent her country at a summit of world leaders. That’s quite a contrast with Donald Trump’s enthusiasm for accepting a Boeing 747 in royal configuration from Qatar. But while Sheinbaum is winning praise for saving taxpayers money, she should have flown business class. The 64 year old president traveled from Mexico City through Tijuana to Shenzhen for the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit. Here’s her departure update: Claudia Sheinbaum’s update on her trip to Shenzhen, November 15, 2026 21 Hours Includes The Stop In Tijuana She left Mexico City early Sunday, November 15, on China Southern flight CZ8032 and arrived in Shenzhen on Monday. The Airbus A350-900 made its scheduled technical stop in Tijuana along the way. The first flight took 3 hours 12 minutes and the transpacific segment clocked in at 14 hours 56 minutes, with about two and a half hours on the ground between them. China Southern operates the route nonstop from Shenzhen to Mexico City, with the Tijuana stop in the other direction. Several stories report that seat 33B is in the back of the aircraft. In fact, it’s the front of standard economy. On arrival she posted video of her welcome in Shenzhen. An airport bus brought her to the red carpet, where an honor guard and performers waited. She received a presidential welcome. Which reminds me a little bit of when I’ve booked first class awards on Lufthansa, but the intra-European segment was only available in coach. Nobody ever could quite figure out how a coach passenger had a vehicle waiting for them on the tarmac. She returned on China Southern flight CZ8031 and shared a photo with the crew: Claudia Sheinbaum thanks the crew of flight CZ8031 on her return from Shenzhen Mexico Sold Its Presidential 787 Sheinbaum is continuing a practice established by Andrés Manuel López Obrador. In July 2020, I wrote about Mexico’s president flying Delta coach and connecting in Atlanta to meet Trump. He had an exit row aisle seat on a Delta flight from Mexico City. Delta was blocking middle seats at the time, too, due to the pandemic. Reforma’s video of López Obrador boarding Delta for his Washington trip, July 7, 2020 López Obrador refused to use Mexico’s presidential Boeing 787-8. After years of trying to find a buyer, Mexico sold it to Tajikistan for about $92 million in 2023. He said the proceeds would fund two hospitals. This isn’t Sheinbaum’s first long trip in coach, either. In April I covered her economy trip to Europe, flying through Madrid to Barcelona and returning to Mexico City on Aeromexico. She posted a photo of herself working at her coach seat: Claudia Sheinbaum working during her April 2026 trip to Barcelona I thought that was performative then. A president’s time has value, and so does being rested for the work the country is sending her to do. Aeromexico business class would have provided better rest and opportunity to work. Pringles, Princes, And A Qatari 747 I’ve covered plenty of politicians and royals flying coach. Prince William flew American Airlines economy from Memphis to Dallas in 2014, in a bulkhead window seat on an Airbus A319. He’s also flown Ryanair and taken the family on Flybe, carrying their hand luggage. Prince Harry and Meghan Markle flew British Airways coach to Nice for New Year’s in 2017. They and three bodyguards occupied the last three rows, with empty seats around them. T Argentina’s Javier Milei flew coach on his trip to Davos in January 2024. Given Argentina’s finances and the spending cuts he wanted the public to accept, there was a political purpose for the symbolism. David Cameron was photographed eating Pringles and working on his iPad aboard easyJet in 2015, while traveling to Portugal. I liked that one. William and Harry’s grandfather Prince Philip, though, described the improvements in air travel over his lifetime with the qualification, “provided you don’t travel in something called economy class, which sounds ghastly.” Meanwhile, thhe Pentagon accepted Qatar’s Boeing 747-8 in May 2025 for Trump’s use as Air Force One. It was widely described as a $400 million gift. I covered the Qatari jet’s conversion this summer, including the decision to preserve its luxury interior and the plan for it eventually to go to Trump’s presidential library. The U.S. president needs secure communications, protection, and the ability to keep working while traveling. That can justify dedicated aircraft. Sheinbaum’s coach trip earned publicity, including favorable attention in China while she was there to meet its leaders. But saving the difference between coach and business class is simply a poor value tradeoff when the cost is productive time and sleep for someone with a high opportunity cost. She left Mexico City early Sunday, November 15, on China Southern flight CZ8032 and arrived in Shenzhen on Monday. The Airbus A350-900 made its scheduled technical stop in Tijuana along the way. The first flight took 3 hours 12 minutes and the transpacific segment clocked in at 14 hours 56 minutes, with about two and a half hours on the ground between them.

China Southern operates the route nonstop from Shenzhen to Mexico City, with the Tijuana stop in the other direction. Several stories report that seat 33B is in the back of the aircraft. In fact, it’s the front of standard economy.

On arrival she posted video of her welcome in Shenzhen. An airport bus brought her to the red carpet, where an honor guard and performers waited. She received a presidential welcome. Which reminds me a little bit of when I’ve booked first class awards on Lufthansa, but the intra-European segment was only available in coach. Nobody ever could quite figure out how a coach passenger had a vehicle waiting for them on the tarmac.

She returned on China Southern flight CZ8031 and shared a photo with the crew:

Claudia Sheinbaum thanks the crew of flight CZ8031 on her return from Shenzhen

I’ve covered plenty of politicians and royals flying coach.

Prince Harry and Meghan Markle flew British Airways coach to Nice for New Year’s in 2017. They and three bodyguards occupied the last three rows, with empty seats around them. T

Argentina’s Javier Milei flew coach on his trip to Davos in January 2024. Given Argentina’s finances and the spending cuts he wanted the public to accept, there was a political purpose for the symbolism.

David Cameron was photographed eating Pringles and working on his iPad aboard easyJet in 2015, while traveling to Portugal. I liked that one.

William and Harry’s grandfather Prince Philip, though, described the improvements in air travel over his lifetime with the qualification, “provided you don’t travel in something called economy class, which sounds ghastly.”

Meanwhile, thhe Pentagon accepted Qatar’s Boeing 747-8 in May 2025 for Trump’s use as Air Force One. It was widely described as a $400 million gift. I covered the Qatari jet’s conversion this summer, including the decision to preserve its luxury interior and the plan for it eventually to go to Trump’s presidential library.

The U.S. president needs secure communications, protection, and the ability to keep working while traveling. That can justify dedicated aircraft. Sheinbaum’s coach trip earned publicity, including favorable attention in China while she was there to meet its leaders. But saving the difference between coach and business class is simply a poor value tradeoff when the cost is productive time and sleep for someone with a high opportunity cost.

NFL great Brady could buy Chelsea stake

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Tom Brady is a seven-time Super Bowl champion

American football great Tom Brady is looking to invest in Chelsea through ownership group Clearlake Capital.

The seven-time Super Bowl winner has recently divested his stake in Championship club Birmingham City, which he bought into following Tom Wagner's Knighthead Capital takeover in 2023.

It now appears he could move into Chelsea, where he has a positive relationship with influential co-owner Behdad Eghbali, having been a star guest at the after-party following the club's Club World Cup triumph in 2025.

Brady would have no voting rights or control at Chelsea, although the ownership group could look to appoint him to the board if it suited all parties.

The move is not said to be linked to Todd Boehly and Mark Walter's Â£950m sale of their stakes to Clearlake Capital, which must be completed by the end of the year.

Meanwhile, Swiss billionaire Hansjorg Wyss remains a significant minority stakeholder at Stamford Bridge.

The investment is also not part of a funding round, according to senior sources, and is not intended to dilute Clearlake Capital's control.

However, other interested parties are said to have approached them about investing since Boehly and Walter agreed a deal to leave.

Former quarterback Brady, a three-time NFL most valuable player, retired in February 2023 after 23 seasons in the NFL, having won six Super Bowl rings with the New England Patriots and one with the Tampa Bay Buccaneers.

Following the end of his playing career he acquired a stake in the Las Vegas Raiders, while also becoming part of the ownership group of the WNBA's Las Vegas Aces.

On top of that, Brady's commitments include him being a lead NFL analyst for Fox Sports.

Arsenal manager Mikel Arteta says he is very happy with Kai Havertz and trusts club chief Andrea Berta to sort out a new contract for him.

The Germany international has been a key player since joining the Gunners from Chelsea back in the summer of 2023, scoring 40 goals in 125 appearances for the club and helping them win the Premier League title.

Still, there has not yet been much of an update on Havertz and a new contract with Arsenal, with the 27-year-old’s current deal set to expire in 2028.

That’s not exactly imminent, but it would still be ideal for Arsenal to get Havertz tied down to a longer contract so as to avoid any uncertainty by the time he has just a year left by next summer, which would lead to his market value tumbling.

The Couple Created a Giant Sunflower Walkway For Their Multicultural Sun Valley Wedding

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Parker Selman and Karan Rai’s love story was born from a sunset. “Our paths crossed a few times before, but the first time we remember speaking was on a roof as we watched the sun go down our senior year at Tufts University,” Parker recalls. A few weeks later, they found themselves alone on a dance floor. “I looked down at my phone to see it was midnight and, therefore, officially my birthday,” Parker says. “I told him he should give me a kiss because it was my birthday, and he did. We were both smitten, and constantly in one another's orbit.” Despite the two lining up jobs on opposite coasts, they quickly realized being practical was not an option, and Karan managed to convince his company to transfer him to New York.

The couple got engaged on a crisp autumn walk around Governors Island—Parker’s favorite spot in the city. She owns and operates a wedding photography business, and as they strolled along, she asked to take a picture of Karan on her medium-format camera with their apartment building and skyline in the background. “While I was snapping the photo, he got down on one knee,” she says, “and I had the privilege of capturing my own proposal.” They rode the ferry back to Manhattan and went to Della’s, a local wine bar, where their families and friends were waiting to celebrate.

Two months later, she proposed back. “While we were walking in Sun Valley, I requested he take a picture of me sitting in the snow with our dog Grumpy,” she says. "I proposed the same way he proposed to me, the moment captured on my Rolleiflex.” Parker used diamonds and emeralds from her late great-grandmother’s engagement ring and gold from Karan’s late grandmother’s earrings to create his signet ring.

Their multi-day wedding unfolded in early September 2026 near Sun Valley, Idaho (where Parker’s family lives). They chose Central Idaho 4-H Camp, located 30 minutes outside of town, for its series of cabins and a large field up in the mountains. “The space was a blank canvas with no sound ordinance,” she says. “Studio Sully transformed the campsite into something magical.”

Going into the planning process, the couple knew they wanted the entire event to feel like a fusion of their cultures. In the days leading up to the Sun Valley celebration, Karan’s parents threw a series of traditional Indian wedding ceremonies and celebrations in his hometown of Pleasanton, California. Once guests arrived in Idaho, they all checked in at The Sun Valley Lodge, where each day the couple had organized group activities.

Festivities kicked off on Friday with a Western-themed welcome party at their favorite après-ski bar, Grumpy’s. Parker wore a tweed corset and skirt by Wiederhoeft accessorized with brown leather Manolo Blahniks and a wooden horse purse by Vin. When the weather cooled down, she tossed on a vintage suede fringe RRL jacket. Karan followed a similar script and wore a Bode suede fringe jacket and One of These Days pants with a painted horse and cowboy detail on the knees. His bandana was made by his mother. “She created a piece inspired by Marie Lichtenberg’s jeweled bandana, utilizing earrings and jewelry of Karan’s grandmothers, mother, and sister,” Parker says.

Guests were encouraged to wear textiles, paisley, and eclectic patterns to evoke a Wild West meets India atmosphere. In one corner, Boise Thread Studio set up shop to embroider initials onto trucker hats, shirts, and bandanas. Meanwhile, guests drank schooners (the name for Grumpy’s beer chalices) on the patio and ate burgers and hot dogs. “Karan and I named our beloved miniature dachshund Grumpy after the bar’s corn dogs, so it is only fitting that we ordered them until they ran out,” Parker says. Her mother welcomed the group with a sunset toast before moving inside. “We took over the electronic jukebox and closed down the restaurant until Karan had to stand up on the booth to usher everyone out the door.”

The next afternoon they hosted a sangeet by the Big Wood River. “The estate has a series of winding paths that lead to the riverside, and we wanted the afternoon portion to feel like a mountain garden party with activities and new discoveries tucked into each corner,” Parker says. The flowing river and live music by Boise-based Ashley Rose Band served as the soundtrack as guests played croquet, horseshoes, and badminton. Near the band, Henna by Sanjana created patterns on guests' hands.

Parker wore Danielle Frankel’s cream Mariela dress featuring two layers of hand-painted wildflowers. “I wanted a long braid with flowers woven in, and Jasmine Burnside helped bring the vision to life,” she says. Karan wore a light blue Jatin Malik sherwani with juttis from his personal collection.

As the sun set, guests were escorted to a clearing where a buffet was set up for dinner, and the dance floor was built around a large willow tree. The vignette was inspired by a 1905 Impressionist painting called Nighttime Festivities that depicts a turn-of-the-century party beneath the trees of a French palace. “I told Melissa [Sully] I wanted the sangeet to be based on this and we became obsessed with bringing the painting to life,” Parker explains. Lights and flowers were strung into the branches that draped overhead. Karan’s parents and Parker’s best friend, Ana, gave speeches before a series of traditional dances were performed by various family members and friends, followed by a lively dance floor courtesy of DJ Anupya.

The morning of the wedding, Parker and Karan got ready with their families and friends. “We are terrible at surprises, and he had seen my dress many times before, so I saw no purpose in a first look,” she says. The couple set off in a convertible (Parker’s hair protected in a vintage scarf from the year she was born), stopping at a vista for photos before arriving at the venue.

For her wedding look, Parker worked with Patricia Voto of One/Of to create a custom gown inspired by Christian Dior’s 1955 Soirée de Lahore. Early on, she knew she wanted something that felt like a combination of Karan and herself. “The original gown was made by the designer two years before his death and was based on a sari with the one-shoulder design and gold embroidery,” she explains. All of the embroidery was done in India, and Parker paired the gown with a custom One/Of veil featuring gold embroidery along the edge, as well as light green shoes by Paula Nadal.

Karan wore a custom Bode tuxedo with a golden sunflower detail, and as a boutonniere, he wore his own emerald kalgi (a traditional Indian headpiece for turbans). “His cufflinks and studs were my late grandfather’s, his cummerbund was unbranded vintage ’70s made in India with embellished sunflowers, and his shoes were suede Hermès.”

Guests arrived to the ceremony dressed in jewel tones and metallics, walking down a pathway with giant sunflowers on either side. Parker and her mother, an amateur florist, had collaborated with Madison of Hart Floral to create the dramatic entrance. “When I spoke of our love for sunflowers and our desire to have them serve as a recurring visual throughout the day, Madison and Melissa joined forces,” Parker explains.

For the ceremony, they stood within a chuppah structure with draped gold chainmail and burgundy sunflower motifs. Karan’s best friend Drew officiated the ceremony, and the fathers of her late friends Alex and Josh read an excerpt by Ovid of the Greek myth of Baucis and Philemon. “Karan’s vows and my own were mirrors of each other. Unknowingly, we made many of the same references, and we each ended by quoting the story of Baucis and Philemon, who, when they died, received the honor of becoming intertwined as trees so they could live on forever.”

Coming from mixed religious backgrounds (Karan’s parents are Sikh and Hindu and Parker’s are Episcopalian and Jewish), the couple chose to honor both. “Karan arrived on horseback as a nod to traditional Punjabi weddings. St. Christopher medallions were placed on each program, and my sister, Alison, shared a short speech about the patron saint of travelers, the namesake of the church we attended as children, and spoke about the new journey Karan and I were embarking upon,” Parker says. “My sister-in-law, Kareena, shared a speech about the jaimala ceremony as a nod to the Hindu wedding tradition, and spoke about the importance of seeing each other as equals.” Karan’s maternal uncle led the group in a Sikh Ardas prayer, and the ceremony ended with the Jewish tradition of the breaking of the glass. As the couple exited, guests threw dried bougainvillea from Karan’s family garden in Chandigarh.

Cocktail hour kicked off with oysters shipped from Island Creek Oyster Co. (the site of the couple’s first two dates). “We really wanted textiles to be an important decor motif throughout the celebration,” Parker says, noting that there was leveled seating overlaid with rugs, a rug-covered bar, and a rug-covered makeshift bandstand. Later on, their sisters shared speeches, and the couple danced to “Can’t Take My Eyes Off You,” then launching into “Dancing in the Moonlight” as tambourines were passed to guests. “Guests followed Karan and I through the field to reveal the tent, set among the cabins.”

The band continued to play as guests entered the tent and danced. At each seat, Parker and Karan placed a letter explaining what their friendship meant to the couple. After the buzz died down, Parker’s father opened the evening with a speech. “We then had a family-style dinner of our favorite bites: kale salad, focaccia, Idaho trout, pesto gnocchi, and vodka meatballs,” she says.

After dinner wrapped up, Parker and her father danced to “Wide Open Spaces” (a song they listened to as she was growing up) before everyone joined in. The couple snuck away to cut the cake—a 30-inch round flourless chocolate confection covered in raspberries—which was passed out on the outskirts of the dance floor. Indian sweets called ladoos were also passed around.

After a full week of wedding events between California for the traditional Indian celebration and four days in Sun Valley, the two were understandably exhausted. “It took weeks to recover, but the whole time all we could talk about was how deeply happy we were.”

Trump FCC declares California isn't allowed to enforce phone service rules

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

FCC preempts state rules that require AT&T to keep offering home phone service.

The Federal Communications Commission ordered California to stop enforcing its Carrier of Last Resort (COLR) rules against AT&T, setting up another battle between the Trump administration and California over the state’s right to enforce its own telecom laws and regulations.

AT&T’s status as a Carrier of Last Resort in California requires it to offer telephone service to any potential customer in its territory. AT&T wants to stop offering basic phone service to new customers and discontinue service for 184,000 residential customers and 15,000 business customers on June 1, 2027.

The order released today granted AT&T’s request to preempt state rules, following up on the FCC’s previous approval of AT&T’s petition to discontinue service. AT&T had tried to achieve the same result in court, but a federal judge rejected its request to override California’s rules in July.

The FCC order probably isn’t the last word, as California could sue the commission. California has stood alone against AT&T, which has received relief from COLR obligations in 20 of the 21 states in its wireline service territory.

“California’s Carrier of Last Resort framework may have made sense when it was established 30 years ago,” FCC Chairman Brendan Carr said in a statement released with today’s order. “But those rules were designed for a bygone era and no longer make sense in today’s competitive, advanced marketplace.”

If AT&T ends service on its old copper lines, many homes and businesses would not be offered modern fiber networks. AT&T claims a wireless home phone service that connects to the cellular network will be good enough, and the FCC agreed.

“The FCC has granted AT&T’s applications to replace legacy voice service with a modern, IP-based alternative in certain areas of the state. California’s state requirements are blocking AT&T from proceeding with a federally authorized transition,” Carr said.

California says its rules allow wireless replacements if they meet the state’s standards for basic phone service, but that AT&T must demonstrate that its wireless phone service would meet the COLR obligations. California’s requirements include flat-rate options for unlimited voice calls, discounted Lifeline rates for eligible low-income customers, and telephone relay service for people who are deaf and hard of hearing.

FCC Democrat Anna Gomez voted against the order, saying that state telecom commissions “are often the last line of defense for consumers. They know their communities, they take their complaints, and they answer to the people who rely on these services in a way that a federal agency in Washington cannot always match.”

Gomez said the California Public Utilities Commission (CPUC) has an open proceeding on how to modernize its COLR rules, and that the FCC should let that process play out. Large technology transitions work best “when the state regulator is a partner with a real role,” but the FCC is treating the CPUC “as an obstacle to be cleared,” she said.

The CPUC “proceeding is the right place for California to weigh what a modern basic service requirement should look like, with input from the people who will actually be making that switch,” Gomez said. “AT&T has said it does not plan to discontinue service before June 1, 2027, which leaves time for the CPUC to finish its work. Rather than wait, this item tells the CPUC to write its new consumer protections around a federal ruling it opposed.”

In March, the FCC issued an order that made it easier for carriers to discontinue copper networks. The order asserted that state rules are subject to preemption if they conflict with the FCC’s discontinuance authorizations and authority. AT&T cited the order in its lawsuit against California.

Trump officials and telecom companies have been trying to block California’s rules for years. The first Trump administration lost an attempt to preempt state net neutrality laws throughout the country in 2019, and California later defeated ISPs in a lawsuit that challenged its net neutrality law in court.

California’s net neutrality law and other telecom regulations are again being threatened by rules the Trump administration applied to federal grants, but California rejected US demands to stop enforcing its laws this month. In that case, California may have to choose between enforcing its telecom laws and receiving $1.42 billion to deploy broadband to 270,571 locations.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

Education Department faces management challenges from recent changes, watchdog says

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

An annual federal report cited interagency agreements and a handful of executive orders as key factors that will challenge the agency in 2027.

Since early 2025, the second Trump administration has outsourced many of the Education Department’s core functions such as grant management, monitoring and technical assistance to other agencies through interagency agreements. The department has also reorganized many of its offices and laid off a bulk of its staff.

"Change of this magnitude can result in challenges associated with employee resistance, fatigue, and uncertainty — such as decreased productivity, operational disruption, and lost efficiency — and have an impact on the organization’s ability to respond to stakeholder questions and concerns," the Nov. 13 report from OIG stated.

The Education Department has not yet issued an annual performance plan for fiscal year 2026 and has yet to issue its annual performance report for FY 2025, strategic plan for FY 2026-2030, or annual performance plan for FY 2027, according to the report.

Other reports through various federal offices have flagged similar concerns with the Education Department's output amid McMahon's efforts to close the agency, which ultimately requires Congressional approval.

In June, another OIG report found that layoffs at the Education Department appear to have impacted units that were performing legal duties.

The staffing changes were made to 16 of the department's 17 offices, resulting in an overall reduction of at least 40% of the more than 4,000 employees who were working for the Education Department when Trump reentered office in January 2025.

Many lawmakers, civil rights groups and lawyers have raised concerns with output from at least one of those offices, the Office for Civil Rights, which saw a slowdown in case resolutions that included no sexual harassment or assault cases being settled in 2025. A later report from the office of Sen. Bernie Sanders, I-Vt., showed that OCR resolved only 1% of its cases overall in 2025, the lowest percentage from any OCR in over the last decade.

Responding to a draft of the most recent OIG report, the Education Department said it had made progress in many of the areas of concern, including change management. According to OIG, the department said it has "continued efforts to improve operational processes and maintain program delivery during a period of substantial organizational change." Additionally, the agency said it has "engaged in contractor support to assist with resolution for external audits," among other adjustments.

The department has previously maintained that its staffing and organizational changes will not affect the statutory functions it is legally obliged to perform.

The department also stressed in a comment to K-12 Dive that the OIG report is an annual update. In a January OIG report, the office flagged similar oversight challenges stemming from executive orders and resulting organizational changes.

"It is critical that the Department strategically plan for and effectively manage these and other major operational changes that it may decide to undertake," it warned at the time.

Past reports have flagged challenges under previous administrations, such as monitoring grantees' use of COVID-19 pandemic relief funding.

Pfizer's Launched and Acquired Drugs Grew 32% in Q3 — and 3 Other Reasons to Buy the Stock Before 2027

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Pfizer remains deeply unloved on Wall Street, but it is slowly turning a corner.

By Reuben Gregg Brewer – Nov 20, 2026 at 5:15PM EST

Pfizer (PFE -0.51%) faces a large patent cliff, and investors worry it won't be able to offset the revenue it loses as older drugs face generic competition. That's a problem, but the drugmaker is aware of it and is making important strides in its efforts to address the issue. The big story, however, is that this issue is pretty normal for a drug company.

In fact, revenue from recently launched and acquired drugs rose 32% in the third quarter of 2026. That shows the pharmaceutical giant's efforts are bearing fruit. But there's more reason to be excited, including the company's strong balance sheet, its large dividend, and management's commitment to continue paying that dividend. Here's what you need to know.

One of the biggest worries about Pfizer is its upcoming patent expirations. Two big ones are Eliquis in 2028 and Vyndamax in 2031. But patent expirations are a normal part of the pharma sector. And research and development efforts in the highly technical and intensely competitive healthcare sector don't always align perfectly with a company's patent expiration schedule. It's just a fact of life.

The 32% revenue growth Pfizer achieved from launched and acquired products is evidence that the company is seeing some success in its efforts to address the issue. And it sets up three more reasons to consider buying the stock.

Pfizer ended the third quarter of 2026 with $1.8 billion in cash and $12.4 billion in short-term investments. That gives the company significant firepower to support its business through this patent-expiration cycle. In fact, the company's cash hoard is slightly higher than at the start of the year, so it isn't rapidly burning through its savings.

Despite the company's results with new drugs and its financial strength, investors are deeply negative on Pfizer's stock. That's highlighted by its huge 6.1% dividend yield as of this writing. To put that yield into context, the S&P 500 index (^GSPC -3.70%) yields roughly 1.1%, and the average pharma stock yields 1.3%.

The negativity highlighted by the elevated yield is echoed by more traditional valuation metrics. Pfizer's price-to-sales and price-to-book ratios are below their five-year averages. And while the price-to-earnings ratio is elevated, it is largely due to one-time charges. Looking at the whole picture, Pfizer's stock looks attractively priced.

Given the lofty yield, Pfizer is likely to be most appealing to dividend-focused investors. But right now earnings aren't covering the dividend. That's not a great sign, but one-time charges are part of the issue. If you look at cash flow, the company can still support the dividend. That's highlighted by a cash dividend payout ratio of roughly 85%. While a lower number would be better, there's still some room for adversity. And don't forget about the company's large cash pile.

Both of these facts help support management's strong backing of the dividend. Indeed, one of Pfizer's key capital priorities is to "maintain and grow our dividend." Obviously, the company's priorities could change, but it wouldn't make a statement like that if the board of directors wasn't on board with protecting the dividend.

At the end of the day, Pfizer is facing very real headwinds today. But it is an industry-leading company with a long history of success. If history is any guide, it will muddle through this difficult period. The success it is having with new drugs, its financial strength, attractive valuation, and dedication to its large dividend are all strong reasons for investors to take a deep dive before 2026 is over. If you wait too long, the opportunity here may pass you by.

Russian strikes damage historic buildings in Kyiv and Odesa, including World Heritage sites

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

St Volodymyr’s Cathedral in Kyiv Photo by Wavemeart, via Wikimedia Commons

Multiple heritage sites in Ukraine were damaged by Russian strikes on 19 and 20 November, including the Unesco-listed St Michael’s Golden-Domed Cathedral and Monastery complex and St Volodymyr’s Cathedral in Kyiv, known for its frescoes, as well as 12 cultural heritage sites in Odesa, the strategic port city on the Black Sea, which was hit a day earlier. The entire historic centre of Odesa was added to Unesco’s list of endangered World Heritage sites in 2023.

At least nine people were killed in Kyiv and other regions of Ukraine during the hours-long attack on 20 November.

“Today, Russian drones damaged one of the most famous monuments of our country, the Volodymyr Cathedral,” Tetyana Berezhna, Ukraine’s deputy prime minister for humanitarian policy and minister of culture, wrote in a Facebook post on 19 November after the cathedral in Kyiv was hit, breaking windows and frames and causing “some damage to the paintings and structural elements”.

The neo-Byzantine St Volodymyr Cathedral, built in the 19th century, is under the jurisdiction of the Orthodox Church of Ukraine (OCU), which broke with the Moscow Patriarchate and was granted autocephaly by Constantinople in 2018. Three of Russia’s most famous artists of the 19th century and early 20th century—Viktor Vasnetsov, Mikhail Vrubel and Mikhail Nesterov—created religious art for St Volodymyr’s, as did the Ukrainian artist Mykola Pymomenko, a teacher of Kazimir Malevich.

The Austrian embassy, located next-door to St Volodymyr's Cathedral, was also damaged. Kyiv's municipal cultural heritage department published an inventory of damage from the attack, which also includes the embassy of the Netherlands.

A Russian drone was shot down over St Michael’s, which is the headquarters of the OCU, on 19 November.

Berezhna’s post cites the cumulative damage from Russia’s accelerating attacks: “Since the beginning of this year, the number of monuments damaged by Russia has increased by more than a third, and by the end of October it was almost 2,200. Almost 2,700 cultural institutions were also affected.”

The minister was in Lutsk, a historic city in northwestern Ukraine, this week to plan for a conference marking the 600th anniversary in 2029 of the Congress of Lutsk, which brought together the regional monarchies of the time. A Russian strike on Lutsk in October damaged Ukraine’s largest painting, Cosmogony, a 2,000 sq. m work by the contemporary artist Petro Antyp.

Unesco has condemned the latest attacks, especially on Odesa, “which hit the centre of the city, within the boundaries of the World Heritage property ‘The Historic Centre of Odesa’” and “reportedly caused damage to several heritage buildings, including the Kalio Palace, a monument of national importance,” according to a statement released by the organisation. The Kalio Palace, built in 1834, is attributed to the Swiss Italian architect Giorgio Torricelli.

The strikes came amid escalating tensions and a diplomatic chasm that appears to be widening. The Russian President Vladimir Putin said this week that the Kremlin would not negotiate any partial ceasefire or deal with Ukraine after it struck and incapacitated the Kerch Bridge linking mainland Russia to Crimea, a symbol of Russia’s annexation of the Black Sea peninsula in 2014, on 16 and 17 November. At the same time, US President Donald Trump claimed that peace, largely on Russia’s terms, is within reach and reportedly urged European leaders to stop funding weapons for Ukraine. Trump, Putin and the Chinese President Xi Jinping signed a “peace declaration” on 20 November at a trilateral summit hosted by Xi in Shenzhen, China.

A petition to “safeguard Ukraine’s cultural heritage and artistic integrity” was launched last month by the curator Konstantin Akinsha and Bjorn Geldof, the artistic director at Kyiv’s PinchukArtCentre. It calls “for immediate sanctions and the suspension of Russian state participation in international cultural events”. Rapporteur, a Brussels-based policy publication, reported this week that ten European countries are seeking to keep Russia out of the 2027 Architecture Biennale. Russia’s participation in the 2026 Venice Biennale set off a series of protests.

The bombing of several buildings including in the historic city centre—a World Heritage Site—has been strongly condemned by Unesco

The 11th-century World Heritage Site was damaged by a strike on Tuesday

A monastery in Kyiv was set ablaze by a missile strike in the latest offensive against the country, with residential buildings also targeted

The UN body is monitoring heritage sites by satellite and plans a meeting with Ukrainian museum directors to safeguard collections

TV Ratings: CMA Awards Score Seven-Year High

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The show delivers its biggest audience of the 2020s so far.

The 60th CMA Awards delivered their largest TV audience of the decade.

The Nov. 18 awards averaged 7.62 million viewers on ABC and Disney+, the best same-day total for the awards since 2019 (when it brought in more than 11 million viewers). Wednesday’s show finished just ahead of the 7.57 million viewers for the 2022 awards — with the caveat that Nielsen has made several changes to the way it measures ratings in the years since then.

The CMA Awards drew a 1.01 rating among adults 18-49 (equivalent to about 1.37 million people in the demographic), the first time the show has cleared a 1.0 rating since 2022. The telecast grew by 26 percent in viewers and 55 percent in adults 18-49 from a year ago (6.04 million viewers, 0.65 rating in the 18-49 demo). Related Stories TV Streaming Ratings: Rough Week for Original Series, 'From' Remains No. 1 Overall Music Taylor Swift Performs "Our Song" and "I Knew It, I Knew You" at the CMAs

Ella Langley set a record with six wins at the awards, including entertainer of the year. Her long-running No. 1 hit “Choosin’ Texas” won CMA Awards for song, single and music video of the year.

Along with Langley’s big night, the CMA Awards featured a performance by Taylor Swift — her first at the ceremony since 2013 — and a tribute to Dolly Parton that featured Vince Gill and host Lainey Wilson performing a duet of “I Will Always Love You.” Several other artists paid tribute to Parton during the show and on the red carpet beforehand.

ABC’s annual Countdown to the CMAs special on Tuesday also earned multi-year ratings highs. Hosted by Robin Roberts, the show drew just under 3 million viewers, its best showing since 2023.

The Venice Biennale's Sham Awards Cannot Artwash Genocide

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

This year’s historic actions demonstrated that Israel’s unspeakable assault on Palestine will not be ignored — and that the power of boycott has never been greater.

This Sunday, the Venice Biennale will award its new “Visitors’ Lions.” But no matter who receives this poisoned chalice, the results are void.

Stripped of the silver and gold they wore in previous iterations of the Biennale, the new Lions have been thrown to the tourists; “Best Artist” and “Best National Pavilion” will be decided by a popularity contest. Why this surprising democratic turn? The expert jury initially appointed to give these honors resigned en masse in April of this year. These resignations were, in part, the result of the Art Not Genocide Alliance’s (ANGA) nearly three years of campaigning to exclude the Israel pavilion from the art world’s most important biennial. After the jury publicly stated their intention to omit Israel and Russia from consideration for the Golden Lions, they were visited by representatives from Italy’s Ministry of Culture. The contents of the meeting are not public, but we can surmise how it went from the jury’s subsequent unanimous resignation. Shortly after, the Venice Biennale Foundation announced the Visitors’ Lions, inviting ticket-holders to cast their vote for awards to be announced at the end of the show’s run.

“Our exclusion has been thwarted, and the choice now passes to the general public,” crowed Israeli Foreign Minister Gideon Sa’ar, whom human rights groups accuse of orchestrating the siege of Kamal Adwan Hospital and the notorious imprisonment and torture of the hospital’s director, Dr. Hussam Abu Safiya, in 2024. Firmly siding with torture and against principle, the Biennale made it clear that both Israel and Russia were back in the running for the Visitors’ Lions. As Antonia Majaca observed, the jury dared to apply a criterion, and the Biennale responded by dissolving judgment itself.

Many artists have rejected this sham from the start. On the day the 2026 Biennale opened to the public, 54 artists in the international exhibition and 22 national pavilion teams withdrew from consideration in solidarity with the jury. In June, more than a hundred artists threatened legal action unless their names were removed from the ballot. Sunday’s ceremony awards a prize that few want to win.

The massive public pushback against the Biennale also demonstrates how close we have come to actualizing the moral principle that a genocidal, apartheid state has no legitimacy on any international stage. This year, we participated in a series of unprecedented protests. Biennale artists and cultural workers went on strike three times in seven months — nothing on this scale has ever happened before (not even during the anti-capitalist protests of 1968, which put a stop to the Biennale’s prizes for 18 years). This year’s protests were also unprecedented in terms of the level of collaboration between ANGA — which comprises artists, curators, and precariously employed workers — and rank-and-file labor unions.

The Biennale is resolutely global, unfolding in the “anywhere or not at all” of the international art circuit. Against the false globalization of the art market, our internationalism was grounded in local organizing against Israel’s Zionist genocide.

The first of this year’s strikes, on May 8, was built locally in Venice, with Biennalocene, Sale Docks, and other local grassroots unions whose participation provided legal protection for striking art workers. On May 8, 30 of the Biennale’s 100 national pavilions closed partially or fully, and thousands of people marched from Via Garibaldi to the Arsenale, where they were met with police brutality. Many artists in the main exhibition draped or altered their works.

The second strike, on June 12, was national in scale. It was the result of a year of organizing assemblies and actions in about 20 cities all around Italy, with organizations including Mi Riconosci?, Vogliamo Tutt’Altro, Biennalocene, and grassroots unions through the Cultural Strike platform.

The third strike, on 30 October, responded to a call that emerged from outside the art world from the World Federation of Trade Unions and, in Italy, was taken up by militant dockworkers, including the Autonomous Collective of Port Workers, and the grassroots Unione Sindacale di Base. A network of port unions at 113 in 22 countries, including Italy, France, Greece, Turkey, and Morocco, refused to load weapons bound for Israel. This was the biggest ever coordinated action of its kind, and our role was to help build local participation in Venice.

Today, November 20, as this piece is being published, the International Student Strike brought further strike action to Venice, demonstrating the political convergence between students and the Biennale — students in the city are often employed in the cultural sector under precarious conditions.

Our ability to do this grew out of the work we began in 2024, in adherence to the guidelines of the Palestinian Campaign for the Academic and Cultural Boycott of Israel (PACBI) and Boycott, Divestment, and Sanctions (BDS), when our open letter demanding Israel’s exclusion from the Biennale was signed by more than 24,000 cultural workers. That year, the Israel pavilion closed.

Between 2024 and 2026, our campaign connected more substantively with the Italian labor movement. As the June 12 strike articulated, the political atomization of artists is a labor issue, related to the art world’s reliance on precarity, which shifts the risk of political action onto individuals. The Biennale provides no fees or production money to artists, leaving them to fundraise their participation. Those whose work is not sufficiently market-friendly to claw back payment in future sales have to content themselves with being paid in the false currency of prestige.

ANGA refuses the oppressive logic that allows the Biennale to support genocide and profit from precarious labor while claiming to rise above politics. This year, we have made clear that the alliance we have created has the power to disrupt the Biennale’s functioning and change its nature.

Today, the contradictions of the present political order — the indigestible, white-supremacy-coded cocktail of Europe’s simultaneous opposition to Russia and support for Israel; the flailing, ever-dying economy; the oceans of money poured into technologies of death and destruction — explode messily into the supposedly morally hermetic market-world of the Biennale.

The farcical “Visitors’ Lions” are the Biennale’s wager that the walls can always be painted pure white again. But this year’s massive actions have demonstrated that we will not allow Israel’s unspeakable assault on Palestine to be art-washed and ignored. ANGA’s members and supporters are no less steadfast than we were three years ago, when our campaign first began. It remains obvious and undeniable that Israel, the United States-backed abattoir-state, must not be allowed to bring its logic of butchery to be art-washed into neutral “complexity” at the Biennale. And we are now considerably more organized and better connected to the wider struggle against Zionism.

When we look back to ANGA’s beginnings, in early 2024, we remember a time when the shock of total Zionist impunity was still raw enough to feel unreal. We could not imagine that friends or colleagues could disagree with this basic principle. We were soon disabused, as many cultural workers washed their hands of blood in service of their careers.

But our collective efforts have transformed this anti-political status quo. The new compositions of struggle made possible by the mass revolt against Zionism offer a model for how the art world can reach beyond the isolated precarity of cultural labor into new alliances — with students, with dockworkers. The power of boycott as a tool has never been greater. We continue to remake the Venice Biennale, the modern world exhibition, from Walter Benjamin’s “place of pilgrimage to the commodity fetish” into a site of struggle.

NYSS is now accepting applications from independent thinkers and experimenters in the arts.

Report: Club to accept €40m offer for French star, Liverpool ready to move

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Liverpool are in the market for another French star and his club is ready to accept an offer.

It's becoming quite obvious that French football has a monopoly when it comes to producing talent. France has become the epicentre of Europe's player development.

The French national team is littered with superstars. Paris Saint-Germain also have some of the best French players at their disposal and are two-time back-to-back Champions League winners.

Liverpool are starting to catch onto the trend. They have lost one French player in Ibrahima Konate but gained three this summer.

Jeremy Jacquet has become a sensational addition. Bradley Barcola has the potential to become one of the best attackers in Liverpool's team.

Meanwhile Djylian N'Guessan is an exciting talent for the future.

Liverpool making exciting moves ahead of JanuaryIt's expected to be a busy winter window for Julian Ward. The club's new sporting director is expected to be keeping a close eye on the French market in particular. Liverpool are eyeing a midfielder and have set their sights on Lamine Camara. There's already an agreement in place over personal terms, and the only thing missing is to negotiate the right price for both clubs. Right now, the two parties may be some distance away from coming up with the perfect compromise in that regard. Liverpool are looking at other players in Ligue 1 as well. Among their shortlist one player who features is Arthur Avom, who could be available for a bargain. Left-back Mahamadou Nagida is also being looked at.
